## 5. Gepersonaliseerde Onderhoudstips

Based on your system analysis, it appears you have a significant amount of disk space consumed by both installed packages and Node.js modules. Let's break down personalized recommendations to help you free up space and maintain your system efficiently.

## Summary of Disk Usage:

*   **Total Packages (estimated):** ~3.7 GB
*   **Total Node.js Modules (estimated):** ~2.7 GB (excluding 0KB directories)
*   **Overall estimated usage:** ~6.4 GB

## Personalized Maintenance Recommendations:

### 1. Review and Prune Large Installed Packages

Many packages are either explicitly installed by you (`manual: True`) or are dependencies (`manual: False`). Focus on the largest ones, especially those you might no longer use.

**High-Impact Candidates for Review (Largest first):**

*   **`google-cloud-cli` (465 MB, manual: True):** This is your single largest installed package.
    *   **Recommendation:** If you are not actively using the Google Cloud CLI, consider uninstalling it. It's a significant chunk of space. If you use it infrequently, you might explore alternative ways to interact with Google Cloud or only install components you need.
    *   **Action:** `sudo apt remove google-cloud-cli` (and `sudo apt autoremove` afterwards).
*   **`google-chrome-stable` (413 MB, manual: True) & `firefox-esr` (277 MB, manual: True):** You have two large web browsers installed.
    *   **Recommendation:** If you primarily use one browser, consider uninstalling the other to free up space. For example, if Chrome is your main, you might not need Firefox ESR.
    *   **Action (example for Firefox):** `sudo apt remove firefox-esr` (and `sudo apt autoremove` afterwards).
*   **`fonts-noto-extra` (334 MB, manual: False):** This is a very large font package.
    *   **Recommendation:** This package provides extra Noto font families for broad Unicode coverage. While a dependency, if you don't work with a wide range of languages or specific applications that require these extensive fonts, its removal might be considered. However, proceed with caution as other applications might depend on it for correct text rendering. Check dependencies carefully before removing.
    *   **Action (Proceed with caution):** `sudo apt remove fonts-noto-extra` (and `sudo apt autoremove` afterwards).
*   **`openjdk-17-jre-headless` (188 MB, manual: False):** This is a large Java runtime.
    *   **Recommendation:** This is a dependency. Determine which application requires Java 17 headless. If you're no longer using any Java applications (especially server-side or headless ones), the application itself could be removed, which would then allow this JRE to be `autoremoved`.
    *   **Action:** First identify and remove any applications that explicitly need Java 17 headless. Then `sudo apt autoremove`.
*   **LibreOffice Suite (various `libreoffice-*` packages, total ~200+ MB):** You have a full LibreOffice installation, including common files, core, writer, calc, draw, impress, base, language packs (`libreoffice-l10n-nl`), and help files (`libreoffice-help-nl`).
    *   **Recommendation:**
        *   If you don't use the full suite, consider uninstalling components you don't need (e.g., `libreoffice-impress` if you never create presentations).
        *   **`libreoffice-help-nl` (27 MB, manual: True) and `libreoffice-l10n-nl` (4.3 MB, manual: True):** If you don't use LibreOffice in Dutch, these can be safely removed.
    *   **Action (example for Dutch help/language):** `sudo apt remove libreoffice-help-nl libreoffice-l10n-nl` (and `sudo apt autoremove` afterwards).
*   **Development Tools (various `*-dev`, `gcc-12`, `g++-12`, `cpp-12`, `libllvm15`, `cmake`, `git`, `gh`):** You have a substantial set of development tools and libraries.
    *   **Recommendation:** These are crucial if you actively code or compile software. If you stop development in a particular language or framework, you can consider removing its associated development packages (e.g., `libicu-dev` if you no longer work with ICU). Be very cautious, as removing core build tools can break your system.
    *   **Action:** Only remove if you are certain you no longer need the specific development capability. For example, if you stop C++ development: `sudo apt remove g++-12 cpp-12 libstdc++-12-dev libgcc-12-dev` (then `sudo apt autoremove`).

### 3. Docker Cleanup (Based on your special instructions)

The analysis does not include Docker usage, but as per your instructions:

*   **Remove dangling images and stopped containers (Safe Default):**
    ```bash
    docker system prune
    ```
    This command will remove:
    *   All stopped containers.
    *   All dangling images (images that have no associated container and are not tagged).
    *   All unused networks.

*   **Remove all unused images and stopped containers (More Aggressive):**
    ```bash
    docker system prune -a
    ```
    **Warning:** This is a more aggressive command. It will remove all of the above, *plus* **all unused images**, including images that were pulled but are not currently used by any container. This might remove images you want to keep for future projects or quick deployment. Only use this if you are sure you want to clear out all non-running Docker assets.

### 4. General System Housekeeping

*   **System Package Cleanup:**
    *   `sudo apt update` (refresh package lists)
    *   `sudo apt upgrade` (install available updates)
    *   `sudo apt autoremove` (remove packages that were automatically installed to satisfy dependencies for other packages and are no longer needed)
    *   `sudo apt clean` (clears out the local repository of retrieved package files)
*   **Remove Old Kernels:** Over time, old kernel versions can accumulate.
    *   **Action:** `sudo apt autoremove --purge` will often clean old kernels. You can also manually list and remove them, but be careful not to remove the currently running kernel.
*   **Empty Trash:** Check your trash folder (`~/.local/share/Trash`).
*   **Downloads Folder:** Review and delete unnecessary files from your `~/Downloads` folder.
*   **Browser Caches:** Clear browser caches and history regularly.
*   **Log Files:** Large log files can accumulate in `/var/log`. While most are managed by logrotate, you can manually check for unusually large files.

By systematically going through these recommendations, starting with the highest impact items, you should be able to reclaim a significant amount of disk space on your system. Remember to back up important data before performing major system cleanups.
